---
**Overview**
Joko Widodo was Indonesia’s directly elected president in 2023, serving a second and final term. He had substantial executive authority, but real power was bounded by the DPR, coalition politics, the Constitutional Court, the military’s continuing influence, regional governments, and, in some policy areas, foreign economic and diplomatic constraints. In 2023 he remained highly active on domestic development, investment, and ASEAN diplomacy, while the year also exposed pressure points around dynastic politics, corruption, press freedom, Papua, and forced relocation conflicts.

**Evidence Records**

1. **Presidential authority, but not unconstrained**
- **Fact:** Jokowi held the presidency in 2023 and remained the central executive decision-maker, but Indonesia’s political system limited him through parliament, coalition bargaining, the courts, the military, and local resistance.
- **Why it matters:** This is the base condition for attributing policy outcomes and failures to him without overstating personal control.
- **Strongest source:** [Freedom House, *Indonesia: Freedom in the World 2024*](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024), published 2024.
- **Locator:** lines 41-43, 68-76, 92-99 in the opened page.
- **Period:** Mostly requested period, with some structural context.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Strong for formal office and moderate for actual influence.
- **Complicating evidence:** The same source notes the military’s influence, party rules favoring large coalitions, and the judiciary’s role in election rules.
- **Cautions:** Freedom House is a high-quality secondary assessment, not a primary legal source.
- **Independent support:** [Presidential Secretariat installation notice](https://presidenri.go.id/siaran-pers/presiden-joko-widodo-dan-wakil-presiden-maruf-amin-dilantik-di-mpr/) and [Freedom House political rights discussion](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024).

2. **Dynastic influence and court legitimacy crisis**
- **Fact:** A Constitutional Court ruling in October 2023 allowed candidates under 40 who had held elected office to run for president or vice president, enabling Jokowi’s son Gibran Rakabuming Raka to become eligible for the vice-presidential ticket; the court’s chief justice, Jokowi’s brother-in-law Anwar Usman, was later found to have committed an ethics violation.
- **Why it matters:** This is one of the clearest 2023 examples of Jokowi’s family and legal institutions intersecting in a way that affected succession politics and public confidence.
- **Strongest source:** [MKRI, *Ethics Council Removes Anwar Usman from Chief Justice Position*](https://en.mkri.id/news/details/2023-11-07/Ethics_Council_Removes_Anwar_Usman_from_Chief_Justice_Position), published 2023-11-07.
- **Locator:** lines 41-45.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Strong for the institutional consequence and family link; weaker for proving direct presidential intervention.
- **Complicating evidence:** Jokowi publicly denied interfering in candidate selection, according to the Presidential Secretariat; Reuters reported the ruling “bolster[ed] speculation” about Jokowi retaining influence.
- **Cautions:** The ethics ruling addresses the judge, not the president; attributing intent to Jokowi requires care.
- **Independent support:** [Reuters](https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/indonesia-court-clears-path-for-presidents-eldest-son-to-contest-2024-ballot-3199223), [Freedom House 2024 Indonesia](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024).

3. **New criminal code narrowed civic space**
- **Fact:** Jokowi signed a new criminal code in January 2023 that HRW and others said threatens freedom of speech, belief, association, women’s rights, and LGBT rights; it also increases the risk of selective enforcement.
- **Why it matters:** This was a major legal-policy move with long-tail effects beyond 2023 and is central to assessing the administration’s rights record.
- **Strongest source:** [Human Rights Watch, *Indonesia: Jokowi’s Term Ends with a Whimper on Rights*](https://www.hrw.org/news/2024/01/11/indonesia-jokowis-term-ends-whimper-rights), published 2024-01-11.
- **Locator:** lines 601-603 and 605 in the opened page.
- **Period:** Later retrospective finding about a 2023 decision.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Strong, because the law was signed during his term.
- **Complicating evidence:** Government statements in early 2023 said it would consult stakeholders on implementation rules, but HRW says those consultations were limited and one-sided.
- **Cautions:** HRW is advocacy-oriented, though usually well documented.
- **Independent support:** [HRW World Report 2024 Indonesia](https://www.hrw.org/world-report/2024/country-chapters/indonesia), [Freedom House 2024 Indonesia](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024).

4. **Press freedom deteriorated under heavy harassment**
- **Fact:** AJI documented 89 attacks and obstruction cases involving 83 journalists, 5 journalist groups, and 15 media outlets in 2023, the highest level in a decade.
- **Why it matters:** This is a concrete indicator of the information environment under Jokowi in his final year.
- **Strongest source:** [AJI, *2023 Indonesian Journalist Safety Situation Report*](https://aji.or.id/data/2-press-freedom-crisis-amid-climate-emergency-and-democratic-erosion-2023-indonesian), published 2024-02-23.
- **Locator:** lines 50-51.
- **Period:** Later retrospective finding about 2023.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Moderate. The data describe the environment during his administration, but not every incident is directly attributable to the presidency.
- **Complicating evidence:** Some incidents were linked to local actors, private security, or nonstate groups rather than central government agencies.
- **Cautions:** AJI is a civil-society monitor; its counts are useful but depend on documentation quality and reporting access.
- **Independent support:** [Freedom House 2024 Indonesia](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024), [RSF Indonesia](https://rsf.org/en/country/indonesia), [Human Rights Watch 2024 Indonesia](https://www.hrw.org/news/2024/01/11/indonesia-jokowis-term-ends-whimper-rights).

5. **Rempang relocation conflict exposed coercive development politics**
- **Fact:** In September 2023, protests over the Rempang Eco City relocation plan turned violent; police arrested 43 people after clashes, and Jokowi said the problem stemmed from poor communication with residents.
- **Why it matters:** This is a clear case where development policy, land rights, coercive policing, and public legitimacy collided.
- **Strongest source:** [Reuters via The Jakarta Post, *Police arrest 43 after riot over Rempang industrial park*](https://www.thejakartapost.com/indonesia/2023/09/13/police-arrest-43-after-riot-over-rempang-industrial-park), published 2023-09-13.
- **Locator:** article body describing the arrests, clash, and Jokowi’s statement.
- **Period:** Requested period.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Strong on policy direction and public response; moderate on direct responsibility for police conduct.
- **Complicating evidence:** Jokowi framed the clash as a communication failure and said residents were to be compensated, which does not resolve the underlying displacement concern.
- **Cautions:** Reuters reporting is strong, but this is a fast-moving event and local conditions were contested.
- **Independent support:** [ANTARA](https://en.antaranews.com/news/293463/conflict-in-rempang-island-arose-due-to-poor-communication-jokowi), [South China Morning Post quoting Reuters](https://www.scmp.com/week-asia/people/article/3234396/indonesias-jokowi-blames-bad-communication-eviction-riots-over-chinese-funded-project).

6. **Corruption problems reached Jokowi’s cabinet and anti-graft institutions**
- **Fact:** By late 2023, six Jokowi-era ministers had been implicated in graft cases; the former communications minister was jailed, the agriculture minister was arrested, and the KPK chair later became a suspect in an extortion case.
- **Why it matters:** This is one of the strongest indicators of governance failure and weak accountability in Jokowi’s administration.
- **Strongest source:** [Freedom House, *Indonesia: Freedom in the World 2024*](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024), plus Reuters reporting on individual cases.
- **Locator:** Freedom House lines 95-99.
- **Period:** Mostly requested period, with some later aggregation.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Strong for cabinet-level responsibility; weaker for proving personal involvement.
- **Complicating evidence:** Anti-corruption actions did occur, including arrests and the president’s eventual dismissal of Firli Bahuri.
- **Cautions:** Cabinet scandals do not automatically prove presidential culpability, but they do reflect the administration’s governance environment.
- **Independent support:** [Reuters on Johnny G. Plate](https://www.theprint.in/world/indonesia-communications-minister-arrested-for-graft/1578700/), [Reuters on Syahrul Yasin Limpo](https://www.theprint.in/world/indonesia-ex-agriculture-minister-named-as-graft-suspect/1800388/), [ANTARA on Firli dismissal](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3891531/jokowi-teken-keppres-pemberhentian-firli-bahuri-sebagai-ketua-kpk).

7. **Economic performance was solid, but not evidence of broad success**
- **Fact:** Indonesia’s economy grew 5.05 percent in 2023, GDP reached IDR 20,892.4 trillion, GDP per capita reached about US$4,919.7, and investment realization reached Rp1,418.9 trillion, above target.
- **Why it matters:** These are the main macroeconomic facts for judging Jokowi’s development agenda in 2023.
- **Strongest source:** [BPS, *Indonesia’s GDP Growth Rate in Q4-2023 was 5.04 percent (y-on-y)*](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2024/02/05/2379/ekonomi-indonesia-triwulan-iv-2023-tumbuh-5-04-persen%E2%80%93y-on-y-.html), published 2024-02-05.
- **Locator:** lines 81-85.
- **Corroborating source:** [ANTARA, *Investment realization of Rp1,418.9 trillion in 2023 surpassed target*](https://en.antaranews.com/news/303858/investment-realization-of-rp14189-trillion-in-2023-surpassed-target), lines 63-70.
- **Period:** Later retrospective finding about 2023.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Moderate to strong for policy environment; weaker for attributing all growth directly to the president.
- **Complicating evidence:** Growth was slower than 2022, and macro gains did not remove rights, corruption, or regional conflict problems.
- **Cautions:** National economic aggregates do not reveal distributional effects or local harm.
- **Independent support:** [BPS poverty profile 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2023/07/17/2016/indonesia), [BPS poverty profile 2024 vs 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2024/07/01/2370/persentase-penduduk-miskin-maret-2024-turun-menjadi-9-03-persen-.html).

8. **ASEAN chairmanship brought diplomacy, but Myanmar remained stuck**
- **Fact:** As ASEAN chair in 2023, Jokowi pushed the bloc to avoid great-power rivalry and pursue peace efforts in Myanmar, but ASEAN still failed to secure progress on the Five Point Consensus.
- **Why it matters:** This captures a major foreign-policy arena where Jokowi had real visibility but limited leverage.
- **Strongest source:** [Human Rights Watch, *Jokowi’s Term Ends with a Whimper on Rights*](https://www.hrw.org/news/2024/01/11/indonesia-jokowis-term-ends-whimper-rights), lines 605-605.
- **Corroborating source:** [Reuters via The Print on ASEAN and Myanmar](https://theprint.in/world/indonesia-warns-asean-on-destructive-rivalry-as-jakarta-summit-opens/1746878/) and [ANTARA on Myanmar trust and the ASEAN summit](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3712083/jokowi-ungkap-adanya-ketidakpercayaan-terhadap-junta-myanmar).
- **Period:** Later retrospective plus contemporaneous 2023 coverage.
- **Connection to Jokowi:** Strong for chairmanship and diplomatic agenda; weak for outcomes in Myanmar.
- **Complicating evidence:** ASEAN’s structural consensus rules and Myanmar’s junta meant Indonesia could not force compliance.
- **Cautions:** Regional diplomacy is noisy and outcome attribution is difficult.
- **Independent support:** [Reuters ASEAN summit coverage](https://theprint.in/world/indonesia-warns-asean-on-destructive-rivalry-as-jakarta-summit-opens/1746878/).

**Information Environment**
- Information was mixed but usable. National macro data from BPS and ministry statements were relatively reliable, while rights, policing, and press-freedom data relied more on civil-society monitoring, international NGOs, and reporting from contested environments.
- The strongest blind spots were Papua, sensitive court politics, police conduct, and forced-relocation disputes. HRW noted restrictions on foreign media, diplomats, and human-rights monitors in West Papua.
- Press freedom conditions were poor enough that AJI reported unusually high attack counts, which likely means undercounting in some categories as well as uneven documentation.
- Court and election-related facts are heavily politicized; the Anwar Usman ethics decision helps because it is a primary institutional record, but motive and intent still require caution.
- Economic statistics are more stable than rights reporting, but they do not show distributional harm, coercion, or local conflict costs.

**Questions For Deeper Research**
- How much direct influence did Jokowi retain over candidate selection, coalition management, and court appointments in late 2023?
- How much of the Rempang conflict was driven by central policy versus local implementation and security-force behavior?
- Which 2023 attacks on journalists or activists can be tied to state actors, and which to private or partisan actors?
- What was the relationship between Jokowi’s development agenda, land acquisition, and rights violations in other infrastructure zones?
- How far did the cabinet corruption cases reflect systemic weakness versus direct presidential tolerance or protection?

--- RESEARCH HANDOFF ---

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 1B ---

**Orientation**
Indonesia in 2023 was a non-nuclear-weapon state with no domestic nuclear arsenal. Jokowi’s government could still shape catastrophic-risk posture through treaty positions, diplomatic messaging, cyber/dual-use governance, and the protection of expert institutions. The strongest 2023 record is not about weapons deployment or command-and-control, but about disarmament diplomacy, treaty alignment, and a modest but real effort to frame nuclear and other catastrophic risks as matters of restraint and prevention.

**Evidence Index**

1. **Indonesia remained a non-nuclear state under safeguards**
- Indonesia was an IAEA member state, party to the NPT safeguards agreement and additional protocol, and party to the Convention on Nuclear Safety and the Joint Convention on Radioactive Waste.
- This is inherited baseline context, not a Jokowi-made change, but it matters because the chapter asks whether he improved a preexisting non-nuclear posture rather than managed an arsenal.
- Strong source: [IAEA Office of Legal Affairs country factsheet](https://ola.iaea.org/Applications/FactSheets/Country/Detail?code=ID)
- Locator: lines 0-27, 41-46
- Lens support: `1B.3`, `1B.4`, `1B.10`
- Attribution: inherited baseline, not directly attributable to 2023 decisions

2. **Indonesia’s 2023 NPT posture emphasized disarmament and negative security assurances**
- At the 3 August 2023 NPT PrepCom, Indonesia said total global nuclear disarmament remained its highest priority and described itself as a state that had renounced the nuclear weapons option.
- This is the clearest 2023 primary-source record of Jokowi-era nuclear-risk rhetoric and diplomacy.
- Strong source: [Kemlu Geneva statement, 3 August 2023](https://kemlu.go.id/jenewa/berita/first-preparatory-committee-meeting-for-the-2026-review-conference-of-the-parties-to-the-treaty-on-the-non-proliferation-of-nuclear-weapons%2C-specific-issue-1%3A-nuclear-disarmament-and--negative-security-assurances%2C-3-august-2023?type=publication)
- Locator: search snippet text and the statement body quoted in the result
- Lens support: `1B.1`, `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`, `1B.10`
- Attribution: direct 2023 diplomatic statement by Indonesia’s delegation

3. **Indonesia pressed SEANWFZ and nuclear-weapon-state assurances**
- Indonesia repeatedly urged Southeast Asia to remain free of nuclear weapons and framed the region’s security through the SEANWFZ treaty and negative security assurances.
- This supports a cautious, anti-escalatory regional posture rather than nuclear normalization.
- Strong source: [Kemlu / SEANWFZ statement by Foreign Minister Retno Marsudi, 11 July 2023](https://setnasasean.kemlu.go.id/siaran-pers/foreign-minister-retno-southeast-asian-region-must-remain-free-of-nuclear-weapons)
- Locator: search snippet text
- Lens support: `1B.1`, `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`, `1B.10`
- Attribution: direct 2023 ministerial statement under Jokowi

4. **Indonesia linked disarmament to deterrence of existential harm, not prestige**
- In its NPT PrepCom statement, Indonesia said security assurances were urgent and called for a universal, unconditional, non-discriminatory, legally binding regime.
- That is consistent with a risk-reduction approach, though it is rhetorical and diplomatic rather than an enforcement record.
- Strong source: [Kemlu Geneva statement, 3 August 2023](https://kemlu.go.id/jenewa/berita/first-preparatory-committee-meeting-for-the-2026-review-conference-of-the-parties-to-the-treaty-on-the-non-proliferation-of-nuclear-weapons%2C-specific-issue-1%3A-nuclear-disarmament-and--negative-security-assurances%2C-3-august-2023?type=publication)
- Locator: search snippet text
- Lens support: `1B.1`, `1B.2`, `1B.4`, `1B.8`
- Attribution: direct 2023 diplomacy

5. **Indonesia kept supporting treaty-based nonproliferation, including nuclear terrorism controls**
- The UN treaty record shows Indonesia is party to the Convention on the Suppression of Acts of Nuclear Terrorism, with a declaration that Article 4 must not be read as legitimizing nuclear weapons use or threat.
- This is a formal legal commitment relevant to preventing proliferation-adjacent abuse.
- Strong source: [UN Treaty Collection, Indonesia declaration under the Nuclear Terrorism Convention](https://treaties.un.org/pages/ViewDetailsIII.aspx?Temp=mtdsg3&chapter=18&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XVIII-15&src=TREATY)
- Locator: declaration text in the treaty record
- Lens support: `1B.4`, `1B.5`, `1B.6`
- Attribution: inherited treaty posture, still relevant in 2023

6. **The government formalized cyber-risk management in 2023**
- Jokowi issued Presidential Regulation 47/2023 on the National Cyber Security Strategy and Cyber Crisis Management.
- This is not nuclear policy, but it is relevant to the chapter’s broader “other catastrophic risks” lens because it shows the administration building national risk-management architecture for a dual-use domain.
- Strong source: [Peraturan.go.id, Perpres 47/2023](https://peraturan.go.id/id/perpres-no-47-tahun-2023)
- Locator: lines 38-48
- Lens support: `1B.9`, `1B.10`
- Attribution: direct 2023 presidential act

7. **The government issued AI ethics guidance in late 2023**
- Kominfo’s Circular Letter No. 9/2023 set out AI ethics principles including safety, transparency, accountability, data protection, sustainability, and a responsibility to manage risk and crisis.
- Again, not nuclear-specific, but relevant to catastrophic dual-use governance and the state’s willingness to regulate emerging risk.
- Strong source: [JDIH Komdigi, Circular Letter No. 9/2023](https://jdih.komdigi.go.id/produk_hukum/view/id/883/t/surat%2Bedaran%2Bspan%2Bclasshighlightmenterispan%2Bclasshighlightkomunikasispan%2Bclasshighlightdanspan%2Binformatika%2Bspan%2Bclasshighlightnomorspan%2Bspan%2Bclasshighlight9span%2Bspan%2Bclasshighlighttahunspan%2Bspan%2Bclasshighlight2023span?search=PERATURAN+MENTERI+KOMUNIKASI+DAN+DIGITAL+REPUBLIK+INDONESIA+NOMOR+6+TAHUN+2025+TENTANG+STANDAR+TEKNIS+DAN+PROSEDUR+PEMBANGUNAN+DAN+PENGEMBANGAN+APLIKASI+SISTEM+PEMERINTAHAN+BERBASIS+ELEKTRONIK)
- Locator: the document body sections on scope, principles, and responsibilities
- Lens support: `1B.9`, `1B.10`
- Attribution: direct 2023 ministerial guidance

8. **No evidence of nuclear weapons acquisition or brinkmanship**
- In the 2023 sources reviewed, Indonesia did not pursue nuclear armament, nuclear threats, or nuclear coercion.
- This is a negative finding, so it is weaker than the positive records above, but it matters because it closes off the main failure mode for this chapter.
- Evidence basis: IAEA treaty status, UN treaty record, and Kemlu disarmament statements
- Lens support: `1B.2`, `1B.3`, `1B.4`, `1B.5`, `1B.6`, `1B.10`
- Attribution: inference from the absence of contrary evidence in the opened record

**Lens Disposition**
- `1B.1` Reducing existential risk: supported by disarmament diplomacy and SEANWFZ posture.
- `1B.10` Lasting risk posture: modestly improved or at least preserved; no evidence of deterioration in nuclear posture.
- `1B.2` Responsible nuclear rhetoric: supported; rhetoric was restraint-oriented, not escalatory.
- `1B.3` Safe nuclear control: largely not applicable because Indonesia had no arsenal; no relevant command-and-control record found.
- `1B.4` Arms control and inspections: supported by treaty participation and disarmament advocacy.
- `1B.5` Nuclear cover for aggression: no evidence found.
- `1B.6` Preventing proliferation: limited evidence; treaty compliance and safeguards support a nonproliferation baseline.
- `1B.7` Independent risk expertise: partial only; cyber/AI guidance exists, but no strong 2023 evidence of a dedicated existential-risk expert apparatus.
- `1B.8` Crisis de-escalation: partial through rhetoric and treaty diplomacy, but no acute nuclear crisis was identified.
- `1B.9` Other catastrophic risks: supported in cyber and AI governance, but this is broader than nuclear risk.

**Information Environment**
- Reliable primary evidence was available for formal treaty status, official rhetoric, and national cyber/AI rules.
- The main limitation is that nuclear-risk evidence is mostly diplomatic and legal rather than operational because Indonesia has no arsenal and no active nuclear crisis in 2023.
- There is also a source asymmetry: the best evidence is official and public, while counterevidence is mostly absence-based.
- Later-retrospective sources were useful for framing but not necessary for the core 2023 claim set.

**Important Follow-up Questions**
- Did Jokowi or his ministers pursue any 2023 institutional reforms that improved Indonesia’s ability to monitor radiological, nuclear, or cyber-catastrophic risks?
- Was the cyber security strategy implemented with funding, staffing, and testing in 2023, or was it mostly formal?
- Did Indonesia’s 2023 SEANWFZ diplomacy produce any concrete movement by nuclear-weapon states on the protocol?
- Were there any domestic expert bodies, simulations, or preparedness exercises for high-consequence biological, cyber, or radiological events in 2023?

- [UN Treaty Collection: TPNW status page](https://treaties.un.org/Pages/ViewDetails.aspx?chapter=26&clang=_en&mtdsg_no=XXVI-9&src=IND)

**Promising Leads Still Needing Inspection**
- UNODA / First Committee records for Indonesia’s 2023 interventions beyond the two Kemlu pages
- CTBTO-related material on Indonesia’s stance toward the CTBT in 2023
- Any 2023 Indonesian defense, emergency, or radiological preparedness budget documents
- Any 2023 academic or audit material on implementation of the cyber strategy and AI ethics circular
- Any 2023 ASEAN or bilateral text showing whether Indonesia pressed nuclear-weapon states on SEANWFZ protocol accession

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 2B ---

**Orientation**
Indonesia in 2023 was not a belligerent in any interstate war, and Jokowi did not face a large-scale external armed conflict to manage. The chapter record is therefore mostly about restraint: ASEAN diplomacy on Myanmar, insistence on international-law boundaries in the South China Sea, and humanitarian management of Rohingya arrivals. The strongest evidence is official and contemporaneous, but the main limitation is that “peace and lawful security” here is largely a diplomacy and crisis-management file, not a wartime command record.

**Evidence Index**

1. **ASEAN chairmanship used to press peace and unity**
- Jokowi opened the 42nd ASEAN Summit by arguing ASEAN should be a driver of peace and growth, not a bystander, and tied that to regional stability and cooperation on health, food, energy, and finance.
- Why it matters: this is the core 2023 foreign-policy frame for peaceful regional order.
- Strongest source: [Setkab, *President Jokowi: ASEAN’s Unity is Key to Peace, Growth*](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-aseans-unity-is-key-to-peace-growth/), 2023-05-10.
- Locator: lines 37-47.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: strong, direct presidential rhetoric and summit leadership.
- Contrary evidence: rhetoric alone does not prove durable peace outcomes.

2. **Indonesia pushed Myanmar implementation and de-escalation**
- Jokowi chaired the ASEAN retreat on Myanmar, said Five-Point Consensus implementation had made no significant progress, and urged a unified ASEAN response, dialogue, an end to violence, and humanitarian access.
- Why it matters: this is the clearest record of active peace-process engagement in 2023.
- Strongest source: [Setkab, *Dipimpin Presiden Jokowi, ASEAN Bahas Tindak Lanjut Five-Point Consensus di Myanmar*](https://setkab.go.id/dipimpin-presiden-jokowi-asean-bahas-tindak-lanjut-five-point-consensus-di-myanmar/), 2023-05-11.
- Locator: lines 46-58.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: strong; Jokowi led the meeting as ASEAN chair.
- Contrary evidence: ASEAN’s own institutional limits meant no decisive breakthrough.

3. **Jokowi explicitly called for stopping violence in Myanmar**
- He said Myanmar’s violence should stop, that no one would win from the current situation, and urged dialogue and a shared solution.
- Why it matters: this is the clearest responsible-use-of-force and de-escalation rhetoric in the 2023 record.
- Strongest source: [Setkab, *Serukan Penghentian Kekerasan di Myanmar, Presiden: Mari Cari Solusi Bersama*](https://setkab.go.id/serukan-penghentian-kekerasan-di-myanmar-presiden-mari-cari-solusi-bersama/), 2023-05-08.
- Locator: search result snippet and article body.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: strong.
- Contrary evidence: none in the opened record.

4. **Indonesia framed Myanmar work as humanitarian and dialogue-based**
- The presidency repeatedly said it was trying to facilitate dialogue, a Joint Needs Assessment through the AHA Centre, and humanitarian aid for Myanmar.
- Why it matters: shows the government chose non-military tools and multilateral channels.
- Strongest source: [Setkab, Myanmar Five-Point Consensus article](https://setkab.go.id/dipimpin-presiden-jokowi-asean-bahas-tindak-lanjut-five-point-consensus-di-myanmar/), 2023-05-11.
- Locator: lines 50-58.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: strong.
- Contrary evidence: humanitarian facilitation did not resolve the conflict.

5. **South China Sea posture was restrained and law-based**
- Jokowi said Indonesia was not a disputing party, supported implementation of a Code of Conduct, and tied regional stability to adherence to UNCLOS rather than coercive claims.
- Why it matters: this is the main 2023 record of lawful security behavior in a maritime flashpoint.
- Strongest source: [The Jakarta Post, *Jokowi rejects South China Sea claims with ‘no basis’*](https://www.thejakartapost.com/world/2023/05/09/jokowi-rejects-south-china-sea-claims-with-no-basis.html), 2023-05-09.
- Locator: article body.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: moderate to strong; based on a contemporary interview reported by a reputable outlet.
- Contrary evidence: no evidence found here of Indonesia taking coercive action.

6. **Rohingya arrivals were handled as a temporary humanitarian issue**
- Jokowi said Indonesia would temporarily accommodate Rohingya refugees and was discussing the issue with UNHCR and other international organizations.
- Why it matters: this shows a legal-security problem was treated as a managed humanitarian and migration issue, not a militarized one.
- Strongest source: [ANTARA, *Indonesia gives temporary stay to Rohingya refugees: Jokowi*](https://en.antaranews.com/news/300759/indonesia-gives-temporary-stay-to-rohingya-refugees-jokowi), 2023-12-11.
- Locator: article body.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: strong, direct statement.
- Contrary evidence: local resistance and unrest complicated implementation.

7. **Rohingya handling was assigned to security and humanitarian institutions**
- Jokowi ordered the coordinating minister for political, legal, and security affairs to handle the issue with local authorities and UNHCR.
- Why it matters: this is the clearest 2023 institutional response to a cross-border humanitarian/security problem.
- Strongest source: [ANTARA, *Presiden Jokowi perintahkan Menkopolhukam tangani pengungsi Rohingya*](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3854268/presiden-jokowi-perintahkan-menkopolhukam-tangani-pengungsi-rohingya), 2023-12-04.
- Locator: article body.
- Period: target period.
- Attribution: strong.
- Contrary evidence: this was reactive crisis management, not a durable settlement.

**Lens Disposition**

- `2B.1` Peaceful alternatives: supported for Myanmar and South China Sea.
- `2B.10` Lasting international peace: partial support only; Indonesia was restrained and lawful, but no durable settlement was achieved.
- `2B.2` Starting or prolonging war: no evidence found of Indonesian initiation, expansion, or prolongation of war.
- `2B.3` Honest justification for force: no relevant force-justification record found; the visible record emphasized dialogue and law.
- `2B.4` Civilian and prisoner protection: partial only through humanitarian aid and Rohingya accommodation; no conflict-prisoner record.
- `2B.5` Control of proxies and arms: no relevant proxy-war evidence found.
- `2B.6` Scrutiny of war claims: partial, through public ASEAN statements and media-recorded interviews; no major obstruction found in the opened record.
- `2B.7` Ceasefires and settlements: partial support on Myanmar process, but no durable settlement.
- `2B.8` Responsible military resources: no material 2023 evidence found that tied military resources to international aggression or intimidation.
- `2B.9` Accountability and remedy: no conflict-specific remedy record found.
- `2B.10` Lasting international peace: only modest support; the best evidence is of restraint, not final settlement.

**Information Environment**
- The record is unusually asymmetric: there is no 2023 Indonesian interstate war record to analyze, so the chapter is dominated by diplomatic text, summit statements, and crisis handling.
- Official Indonesian sources are strong for what Jokowi said and chaired.
- Reuters-style reporting is useful for independent corroboration on South China Sea and regional diplomacy.
- The main limitation is that Myanmar and Rohingya issues were indirect and multilateral, so attribution to Jokowi is about leadership and policy direction, not sole control over outcomes.

**Questions For Deeper Research**
- Did Indonesia’s 2023 Myanmar diplomacy produce any concrete access, aid, or de-escalation gains on the ground?
- Did Jokowi’s South China Sea posture translate into any operational maritime restraint or coordination measures?
- How much of the Rohingya response was humanitarian versus securitized border control?
- Were there any 2023 defense or foreign-policy decisions that tied Indonesia more closely to a regional balancing role, beyond rhetoric?

**Corroborating Sources Opened**
- [Setkab ASEAN unity speech](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-aseans-unity-is-key-to-peace-growth/)
- [Setkab Myanmar Five-Point Consensus meeting](https://setkab.go.id/dipimpin-presiden-jokowi-asean-bahas-tindak-lanjut-five-point-consensus-di-myanmar/)
- [Setkab Myanmar violence statement](https://setkab.go.id/serukan-penghentian-kekerasan-di-myanmar-presiden-mari-cari-solusi-bersama/)
- [The Jakarta Post on South China Sea remarks](https://www.thejakartapost.com/world/2023/05/09/jokowi-rejects-south-china-sea-claims-with-no-basis.html)
- [ANTARA Rohingya temporary stay](https://en.antaranews.com/news/300759/indonesia-gives-temporary-stay-to-rohingya-refugees-jokowi)
- [ANTARA Rohingya handling order](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3854268/presiden-jokowi-perintahkan-menkopolhukam-tangani-pengungsi-rohingya)
- [Reuters ASEAN/Myanmar coverage via Investing.com](https://www.investing.com/news/world-news/myanmars-crisis-overshadows-asean-summit-in-indonesia-3077916)

**Promising Leads Still Needing Inspection**
- ASEAN official summit communiqués and chair statements on Myanmar and the Indo-Pacific
- UNHCR or IOM material on 2023 Rohingya arrivals and Indonesian handling
- Any 2023 defense-white-paper or foreign-ministry record on South China Sea implementation or maritime patrol restraint
- OHCHR or UN Special Procedures material on whether Indonesia’s treatment of Rohingya became a broader human-rights issue

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 3B ---

**Orientation**
Indonesia in 2023 was not in civil war, but domestic safety was shaped by two hard realities: recurring state violence in Papua and protest policing incidents elsewhere, plus a weak accountability environment for security actors. Jokowi had formal command over the national executive, police, and military leadership, but much of the abuse record came from decentralized or semi-autonomous enforcement behavior that the presidency could influence only through appointments, discipline, investigation, and public direction. The 2023 record shows some restraint in rhetoric and a few accountability actions, but also persistent impunity, limited remedy, and repeated allegations of excessive force.

**Compact Evidence Index**

1. **Baseline of continuing physical-integrity abuse**
- 2023 human-rights reporting still described extrajudicial killings, arbitrary arrests, torture, and impunity as recurring issues in Indonesia, especially in Papua.
- Why it matters: establishes that Jokowi inherited a serious domestic safety problem rather than a clean slate.
- Strong source: [U.S. State Department 2023 Country Report on Human Rights Practices: Indonesia](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107730.html)
- Fit: inherited baseline and target-period context.
- Lens support: `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.10`, `3B.6`

2. **Papua remained the main state-violence hotspot**
- UN human-rights experts continued to question excessive force against Indigenous Papuans, and open-source reporting in 2023 described lethal violence and arrests in Papua-linked security incidents.
- Why it matters: this was the most important domestic safety theater in 2023.
- Strong source: [OHCHR Special Procedures communication summary on alleged extrajudicial killings of indigenous Papuans](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TmSearch/SummaryPrint?id=24202)
- Fit: target-period, with longer-running inherited context.
- Lens support: `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.6`, `3B.10`

3. **Security-force abuse in Wamena**
- Komnas HAM said there were indications of apparatus involvement in the Wamena riot that killed 12 people.
- Why it matters: suggests state actors may have contributed to lethal disorder rather than simply restoring order.
- Strong source: [Kompas.com on Komnas HAM’s findings](https://nasional.kompas.com/read/2023/03/03/18360451/komnas-ham-ungkap-dugaan-keterlibatan-aparat-dalam-kerusuhan-wamena-yang)
- Fit: target-period.
- Lens support: `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.9`

4. **Nduga and Papua remained high-risk zones**
- Reporting in 2023 described killings in Nduga and pressure for investigation into state force use.
- Why it matters: this points to recurring insecurity rather than isolated events.
- Strong source: [Kompas.com on Amnesty’s call to investigate killings in Nduga](https://nasional.kompas.com/read/2023/08/22/00054321/amnesty-internasional-desak-polisi-dan-komnas-ham-usut-tuntas-penembakan-3)
- Fit: target-period.
- Lens support: `3B.1`, `3B.2`, `3B.4`, `3B.6`, `3B.7`

5. **Rempang protest showed coercive crowd control around development policy**
- A September 2023 land-relocation protest escalated into violence, with police arresting 43 people.
- Why it matters: this is one of the clearest examples of security force action around a domestic crisis affecting physical safety.
- Strong source: [Reuters via The Jakarta Post on Rempang arrests](https://www.thejakartapost.com/indonesia/2023/09/13/police-arrest-43-after-riot-over-rempang-industrial-park)
- Fit: target-period.
- Lens support: `3B.2`, `3B.6`, `3B.8`, `3B.9`

6. **Journalist safety was poor and police violence was recurring**
- AJI documented 89 attacks and obstruction cases against journalists and media in 2023, with police implicated in multiple incidents.
- Why it matters: journalistic safety is a direct indicator of the climate for peaceful dissent and public accountability.
- Strong source: [AJI 2023 journalist safety report](https://aji.or.id/data/2-press-freedom-crisis-amid-climate-emergency-and-democratic-erosion-2023-indonesian)
- Fit: target-period, later-retrospective reporting.
- Lens support: `3B.2`, `3B.3`, `3B.4`, `3B.8`, `3B.10`

7. **Legal and oversight channels existed, but remedy was limited**
- The UN Human Rights Committee’s Indonesia review and communications show that abuse claims were being raised internationally, which is evidence that domestic remedies were not fully trusted or adequate.
- Why it matters: speaks to independent oversight and remedy.
- Strong source: [OHCHR Special Procedures communications index for Indonesia](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TmSearch/Mandates?m=31&sort=country)
- Fit: target-period context.
- Lens support: `3B.4`, `3B.6`, `3B.9`

8. **Domestic surveillance risks existed**
- Amnesty’s 2024 follow-up research documented spyware-export concerns in Indonesia, indicating domestic surveillance capacity and opacity around monitoring tools.
- Why it matters: not a direct 2023 incident record, but it is relevant to the fear climate and control of dissent.
- Strong source: [Amnesty Security Lab, *A Web of Surveillance*](https://securitylab.amnesty.org/latest/2024/05/a-web-of-surveillance)
- Fit: later-retrospective.
- Lens support: `3B.5`, `3B.8`, `3B.10`

9. **No evidence of a nationwide state-terror regime in 2023**
- The record reviewed shows serious local and regional abuse, but not a national system of mass terror on the scale of wartime or coup-era repression.
- Why it matters: helps distinguish severe abuse from totalizing fear.
- Basis: synthesis of the opened record.
- Fit: inference from target-period sources.
- Lens support: `3B.6`, `3B.10`

10. **Vulnerable groups were not evenly protected**
- Papua, Indigenous communities, protest participants, and journalists were clearly more exposed than the general population.
- Why it matters: chapter relevance depends on unequal exposure and uneven protection, not just aggregate safety.
- Basis: synthesis from the opened record.
- Fit: target-period and inherited context.
- Lens support: `3B.7`, `3B.10`

**Lens Disposition**

- `3B.1` Protection from state abuse: partial support, but the record is adverse overall.
- `3B.10` Lasting domestic safety: weak-to-negative; some restraint, but no durable safety improvement.
- `3B.2` Control of coercive forces: weak; repeated abuse and weak discipline signals.
- `3B.3` Incitement and targeted hatred: limited evidence; no major presidential incitement found in the inspected record.
- `3B.4` Complaints and independent oversight: partial; oversight existed, but remedy was incomplete and often slow.
- `3B.5` Emergency and surveillance powers: partial concern; surveillance opacity is a risk, but no single 2023 emergency-capture event dominated the record.
- `3B.6` Safety without state terror: mixed; violence was not regime-wide terror, but fear and coercion were real in Papua and protest settings.
- `3B.7` Protection of vulnerable groups: weak; exposure was clearly uneven and protection incomplete.
- `3B.8` Safe protest and dissent: weak; journalists and protesters faced police violence and obstruction.
- `3B.9` Restrained crisis response: weak-to-mixed; some responses were reactive and coercive rather than clearly restrained and remedial.

**Information Environment**
- The best evidence comes from rights reporting, UN communications, and civil-society monitoring, because direct official accounts often omit abuse detail.
- Papua is the hardest area to assess: access constraints, intimidation, and contested reporting make undercounting likely.
- Journalist safety data are unusually useful because incidents are concrete and cumulative.
- Some sources are retrospective summaries, which are helpful for pattern detection but weaker for precise incident chronology.
- The biggest gap is a clean, official, outcome-verified accounting of police and military discipline in response to abuse allegations.

**Questions For Deeper Research**
- Which 2023 killings, injuries, or arrests in Papua were confirmed by independent investigation versus allegation?
- How often did Jokowi’s government discipline officers after abuse claims, and what sanctions were actually enforced?
- Did any 2023 security policy materially improve protest safety outside Papua?
- What was the role of the military versus police in the worst domestic safety incidents?
- Were vulnerable groups outside Papua, especially women and migrants, similarly exposed but underreported?

**Labelled Source Groups**

**Accepted evidence**
- [U.S. State Department / ecoi Indonesia 2023 human-rights report](https://www.ecoi.net/en/document/2107730.html)
- [OHCHR communication summary on alleged extrajudicial killings of indigenous Papuans](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TmSearch/SummaryPrint?id=24202)
- [Kompas.com on Wamena and Komnas HAM](https://nasional.kompas.com/read/2023/03/03/18360451/komnas-ham-ungkap-dugaan-keterlibatan-aparat-dalam-kerusuhan-wamena-yang)
- [Kompas.com on Nduga killings](https://nasional.kompas.com/read/2023/08/22/00054321/amnesty-internasional-desak-polisi-dan-komnas-ham-usut-tuntas-penembakan-3)
- [Reuters via The Jakarta Post on Rempang](https://www.thejakartapost.com/indonesia/2023/09/13/police-arrest-43-after-riot-over-rempang-industrial-park)
- [AJI 2023 journalist safety report](https://aji.or.id/data/2-press-freedom-crisis-amid-climate-emergency-and-democratic-erosion-2023-indonesian)

**Corroborating / context sources**
- [OHCHR Special Procedures communications index for Indonesia](https://spcommreports.ohchr.org/TmSearch/Mandates?m=31&sort=country)
- [UN Human Rights Committee Indonesia page](https://tbinternet.ohchr.org/_layouts/15/treatybodyexternal/SessionDetails1.aspx?Lang=en&SessionID=2703)
- [RSF Indonesia 2023 analysis](https://rsf.org/en/analyse_regionale/965)
- [Amnesty Security Lab spyware research](https://securitylab.amnesty.org/latest/2024/05/a-web-of-surveillance)
- [KontraS police accountability report 2024](https://kontras.org/laporan/laporan-hari-bhayangkara-2024-reformasi-polisi-tinggal-ilusi)

**Uninspected or weak leads**
- The 2023 domestic human-rights treaty-body materials in the OHCHR record need direct page-level extraction.
- The BPK and court materials may help on accountability, but the current view did not yield chapter-usable facts.
- Additional Papua-specific local-language reporting would likely sharpen attribution and scale.

**Source Counts and Gaps**
- Strong evidence records retained: 6
- Context/corroboration records retained: 4
- Rejected or unusable leads: 2
- Main remaining gap: a verified, officer-level accountability trail for 2023 abuse cases, especially Papua and protest policing.

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 4B ---

**Orientation**
Indonesia in 2023 remained electoral and formally competitive, but the year’s record points to democratic backsliding pressures rather than democratic deepening. Jokowi still held real executive leverage, yet political freedom was constrained by entrenched coalition politics, elite capture of institutions, the Constitutional Court episode around Gibran, pressure on media and dissent, and a highly personalized governing style that blurred neutrality near the 2024 succession. The strongest 2023 evidence is not that elections disappeared, but that contestation remained real while the field was increasingly tilted.

**Compact Evidence Index**

1. **Competitive elections still existed, but the field was not neutral**
- Indonesia had real opposition competition, but the 2023–2024 electoral environment was shaped by elite bargaining, institutional tilt, and a high-risk succession process.
- Best anchor: [Freedom House, *Indonesia: Freedom in the World 2024*](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024)
- Support for `4B.1`, `4B.4`, `4B.5`, `4B.8`, `4B.10`

2. **Constitutional Court ruling and dynastic politics damaged institutional legitimacy**
- The October 2023 age-rule ruling that opened the door for Gibran’s candidacy became a major test of rule-based competition.
- Best anchor: [MKRI ethics decision on Anwar Usman](https://en.mkri.id/news/details/2023-11-07/Ethics_Council_Removes_Anwar_Usman_from_Chief_Justice_Position)
- Support for `4B.2`, `4B.4`, `4B.5`, `4B.8`, `4B.10`

3. **Opposition, press, and civic space faced pressure**
- Journalists, protesters, and watchdogs faced harassment, and press safety worsened in 2023.
- Best anchors: [AJI 2023 journalist safety report](https://aji.or.id/data/2-press-freedom-crisis-amid-climate-emergency-and-democratic-erosion-laporan-situasi-keamanan-jurnalis-indonesia-2023) and [Freedom on the Net 2023](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-net/2023)
- Support for `4B.3`, `4B.6`, `4B.9`, `4B.10`

4. **Election rules were formally amended during the cycle**
- PKPU and related electoral adjustments in 2023 became part of the wider controversy over fairness and institutional manipulation.
- Best anchor: [KPU Regulation No. 23/2023](https://jdih.kpu.go.id/peraturan-kpu/detail/t4qGNKHcJDug2fRyd06zu0p6TVh0TFlWOHJUL0NtdUxkYk5xTnc9PQ)
- Support for `4B.2`, `4B.4`, `4B.8`

5. **Digital freedom remained constrained**
- Indonesia continued to show a mixed online environment with pressure on content, chilling effects, and state leverage over the information space.
- Best anchor: [Freedom House, *Freedom on the Net 2023: Indonesia*](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-net/2023)
- Support for `4B.6`, `4B.9`, `4B.3`

6. **Independent checks existed but were stressed**
- Courts, auditors, and election institutions did not collapse, but the 2023 record shows they were not strong enough to prevent legitimacy controversy around succession politics.
- Best anchors: [UN treaty-body materials on Indonesia](https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=ixgYqwSUNcJMsTKZgHYdiJEacDGm2y%2BkATxoRQHWzH1gZ9IIVl%2B%2Fe5%2BKwCRLzmUtyvnKiCGobsI%2BTI5%2Fd1rxmA%3D%3D), [Freedom House 2024]
- Support for `4B.4`, `4B.5`, `4B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `4B.1` Genuine electoral choice: partial; elections were competitive, but not cleanly neutral.
- `4B.10` Lasting democratic resilience: negative-to-mixed; democratic form remained, but resilience weakened.
- `4B.2` No entrenchment of power: adverse; the succession controversy is the central problem.
- `4B.3` Opposition and civic freedom: adverse; civic space remained open but pressured.
- `4B.4` Independent checks on power: mixed; institutions existed, but their constraining power was limited.
- `4B.5` Politically neutral institutions: adverse; appointments and institutional behavior looked increasingly politicized.
- `4B.6` Independent information and media: adverse; media pressure and digital constraints persisted.
- `4B.7` Equal political rights: partial only; formal equality existed, but access and safety were uneven.
- `4B.8` Term limits and transfer: mixed; term limits held, but succession fairness was disputed.
- `4B.9` Digital freedom and surveillance: adverse; surveillance and administrative control concerns remained.

**Information Environment**
- Good macro evidence exists from Freedom House, KPU rules, and the court ethics decision.
- The weakest area is direct evidence of internal decision-making around Jokowi’s intent. That has to be inferred from surrounding institutional effects, not assumed.
- Press-freedom and digital-freedom evidence is stronger than evidence on hidden elite bargaining, which is naturally harder to document.
- The 2023 account is therefore best read as institutional tilt and erosion under formal democracy, not outright closure.

**Remaining Research Gaps**
- What exact role did Jokowi play, if any, in the election-rule and succession decisions?
- Which 2023 media or civic-space violations were centrally directed versus locally driven?
- Did any 2023 election-administration remedies meaningfully improve neutrality after the Gibran controversy?
- How much did digital surveillance or content-control practices directly affect political opposition in 2023?

- [Freedom House 2024 Indonesia](https://freedomhouse.org/country/indonesia/freedom-world/2024)

- [KPU Regulation No. 23/2023](https://jdih.kpu.go.id/peraturan-kpu/detail/t4qGNKHcJDug2fRyd06zu0p6TVh0TFlWOHJUL0NtdUxkYk5xTnc9PQ)
- [UN treaty-body material on Indonesia](https://docstore.ohchr.org/SelfServices/FilesHandler.ashx?enc=ixgYqwSUNcJMsTKZgHYdiJEacDGm2y%2BkATxoRQHWzH1gZ9IIVl%2B%2Fe5%2BKwCRLzmUtyvnKiCGobsI%2BTI5%2Fd1rxmA%3D%3D)

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 5B ---

**Orientation**
Indonesia’s 2023 economic record under Jokowi is better described as growth with unresolved distributional and institutional weaknesses than as a clean prosperity story. Macro indicators were positive: growth stayed around 5 percent, inflation was contained, investment exceeded target, and the government kept pushing downstream industrial policy and infrastructure. But the strongest 2023 evidence also shows persistent inequality, uneven regional development, a high degree of policy personalization, and mixed evidence that public investment translated into broad welfare gains.

**Compact Evidence Index**

1. **Macroeconomic stability held**
- Indonesia kept growth above 5 percent in 2023, with inflation comparatively controlled and investment realization above target.
- Why it matters: this is the baseline for judging whether Jokowi preserved stability.
- Best anchors: [BPS GDP growth release](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2024/02/05/2379/ekonomi-indonesia-triwulan-iv-2023-tumbuh-5-04-persen%E2%80%93y-on-y-.html) and [ANTARA investment realization report](https://en.antaranews.com/news/303858/investment-realization-of-rp14189-trillion-in-2023-surpassed-target)
- Supports `5B.3`, `5B.9`, `5B.10`

2. **Economic policy centered on downstreaming and investment**
- Jokowi continued to push mineral and broader natural-resource downstreaming as the core development strategy.
- Why it matters: this is the main formal growth model of the administration.
- Best anchors: [ANTARA on downstreaming policy](https://en.antaranews.com/news/290712/no-country-can-stop-indonesias-downstreaming-policy-jokowi) and [ANTARA on expanding downstream industry](https://en.antaranews.com/news/273621/homepage-v2.html)
- Supports `5B.1`, `5B.4`, `5B.6`, `5B.10`

3. **Public investment was substantial but uneven in payoff**
- Large public projects and state-guided investment remained central, but some priorities were long-horizon and difficult to evaluate by 2023 outcomes alone.
- Why it matters: prosperity claims depend on whether spending created durable public capital or just announcements.
- Best anchors: [UN Indonesia annual report 2023 page](https://indonesia.un.org/en/278982-indonesia-un-launched-annual-report-highlighting-2023-contributions-indonesia%E2%80%99s-development) and BPS / ministry releases
- Supports `5B.6`, `5B.7`, `5B.10`

4. **Distribution remained uneven**
- Growth did not eliminate inequality, and gains were not evenly shared across regions or classes.
- Why it matters: the chapter is about well-being, not GDP alone.
- Best anchors: local WDI / inequality context from structured package and regional poverty / inequality studies
- Supports `5B.8`, `5B.10`

5. **The government was not a pure meritocracy**
- The economic leadership team was competent in formal terms, but policy making was still highly centralized and politically managed.
- Why it matters: this affects whether the record shows technocratic capacity or loyalty politics.
- Best anchors: cabinet and central-bank context, plus policy synthesis materials
- Supports `5B.2`, `5B.3`, `5B.7`

6. **There were real gains in investment and infrastructure, but unclear broad welfare conversion**
- Investment realization exceeded target, but the evidence does not show that this translated into equally strong median household gains everywhere.
- Why it matters: broad prosperity is not the same as aggregate capital inflow.
- Best anchors: [ANTARA investment realization](https://en.antaranews.com/news/303858/investment-realization-of-rp14189-trillion-in-2023-surpassed-target), national statistics, and regional studies
- Supports `5B.6`, `5B.8`, `5B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `5B.1` Broad and sustainable prosperity: partial support. The policy direction was growth-oriented, but evidence of broad, sustainable benefit is mixed.
- `5B.10` Lasting economic trajectory: mixed-positive. Stability and investment were preserved, but distributional and structural questions remain.
- `5B.2` Qualified economic leadership: partial support. Leadership was professionally staffed overall, but not fully insulated from politics.
- `5B.3` Macroeconomic stability: supported. Growth, inflation control, and investment performance were solid in 2023.
- `5B.4` Fair economic rules: partial. The formal framework supported business activity, but enforcement and predictability were uneven.
- `5B.5` Resistance to economic capture: mixed-to-weak. Anti-corruption and procurement concerns remained; stronger capture evidence is needed for a definitive adverse conclusion.
- `5B.6` Productive public investment: partial support. Investment was large, but payoff quality and distribution are unevenly documented.
- `5B.7` Evidence and correction: partial. Official data were published and usable, but correction of weak programs was not always clear.
- `5B.8` Fair distribution: weak-to-mixed. Gains were not equally shared across regions and groups.
- `5B.9` Managing economic shocks: supported at the macro level. Indonesia handled 2023 without major macro instability.

**Information Environment**
- The strongest evidence is from BPS and related official macro data, which are relatively reliable for aggregate performance.
- The hardest part is proving welfare transmission: GDP, investment, and downstreaming do not by themselves show household prosperity.
- Some potentially useful sources in the catalogue are bibliographic only or too broad to yield exact 2023 claims without deeper extraction.
- The record is strongest on macro management and weaker on distribution, capture, and the actual quality of public capital formation.

**Remaining Research Gaps**
- What was the median-household and regional experience of 2023 growth?
- Which downstreaming projects produced measurable productivity gains by year-end 2023?
- How much of investment performance was foreign versus domestic, and how much was policy-driven versus commodity-cycle driven?
- Were there notable 2023 cases of procurement favoritism, capture, or weak competition tied to growth policy?
- Did public spending materially improve welfare services, or mainly support headline infrastructure and industrial targets?

**Accepted / Core Sources**
- [BPS GDP growth release](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2024/02/05/2379/ekonomi-indonesia-triwulan-iv-2023-tumbuh-5-04-persen%E2%80%93y-on-y-.html)
- [ANTARA investment realization report](https://en.antaranews.com/news/303858/investment-realization-of-rp14189-trillion-in-2023-surpassed-target)
- [ANTARA on downstreaming policy](https://en.antaranews.com/news/290712/no-country-can-stop-indonesias-downstreaming-policy-jokowi)
- [ANTARA on downstream industry expansion](https://en.antaranews.com/news/273621/homepage-v2.html)
- [UN Indonesia annual report 2023 page](https://indonesia.un.org/en/278982-indonesia-un-launched-annual-report-highlighting-2023-contributions-indonesia%E2%80%99s-development)
- [BPS and related structured local package facts from the parent workflow]

**Promising Leads**
- [Indonesia Country Report 2026, BTI](https://bti-project.org/en/reports/country-report/IDN)
- [INDEF 1-decade evaluation seminar](https://indef.or.id/en/evaluasi-1-dekade-pemerintahan-jokowi)
- [UGM thesis on infrastructure and inequality](https://etd.repository.ugm.ac.id/penelitian/detail/247536)
- [UGM thesis on social spending and poverty in Nusa Tenggara](https://etd.repository.ugm.ac.id/penelitian/detail/248012)
- [UGM thesis on stunting and health spending under Jokowi](https://etd.repository.ugm.ac.id/penelitian/detail/244888)

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 6B ---

**Orientation**
Indonesia’s 2023 social-wellbeing record under Jokowi is mixed: the state kept funding health, social protection, and poverty-reduction programs, and aggregate poverty and stunting indicators improved in some measures, but access and quality remained uneven across regions and groups. The clearest 2023 facts are about program priority and social spending, not a fully equalized welfare state. The main caution is attribution: some gains reflect post-pandemic normalization, local delivery, and baseline trends, not presidential control alone.

**Compact Evidence Index**

1. **Welfare was a stated budget priority**
- Jokowi’s 2023 budget and government messaging continued to prioritize social protection, education, and poverty reduction.
- Best anchors: [Setkab on 2023 budget priorities](https://setkab.go.id/en/govt-continues-to-prioritize-social-protection-program-education-on-2023-state-budget) and [President Jokowi delivers details of the 2023 State Budget](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-delivers-details-of-the-2023-state-budget)
- Supports `6B.1`, `6B.2`, `6B.6`, `6B.9`

2. **Health and social protection were institutionally expanded**
- The administration kept pushing social insurance and health-system support, including the social-security roadmap and health-spending emphasis.
- Best anchors: [Perpres 36/2023 on the social security roadmap](https://peraturan.bpk.go.id/Search?p=1&tema=118), [BMJ Open UHC study](https://bmjopen.bmj.com/content/11/10/e050565)
- Supports `6B.2`, `6B.4`, `6B.9`

3. **Poverty and stunting remained central social-policy targets**
- Jokowi repeatedly pressed regional governments to reduce stunting and extreme poverty, and BPS continued to publish poverty profiles and welfare statistics.
- Best anchors: [Setkab on stunting/extreme poverty](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-urges-regional-governments-to-reduce-stunting-extreme-poverty) and [BPS poverty profile 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2023/07/17/2016/indonesia)
- Supports `6B.3`, `6B.5`, `6B.6`, `6B.10`

4. **Welfare access was uneven and implementation-dependent**
- Public-service and social-protection delivery still varied significantly by region, with monitoring bodies noting problems in targeting and consistency.
- Best anchors: [Ombudsman material on social aid consistency](https://ombudsman.go.id/perwakilan/news/r/pwkinternal--inkonsistensi-bantuan-sosial) and [public service report](https://ombudsman.go.id/artikel/r/pwkinternal--rapor-pelayanan-publik-pemerintah-daerah-tahun-2023)
- Supports `6B.2`, `6B.3`, `6B.7`, `6B.9`

5. **Household welfare improved in some measures, but inequality and deprivation persisted**
- BPS welfare and poverty publications show a system with persistent gaps rather than uniform gains.
- Best anchors: [BPS Welfare Statistics 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/publication/2023/10/27/bc5b86dd8f16cd0640aa278b/statistik-kesejahteraan-rakyat-2023.html), [BPS Welfare Indicators 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/publication/2023/11/06/7807339c2dfaed0ca8e0beaa), [BPS poverty data by district/municipality](https://www.bps.go.id/en/publication/2023/11/30/587f63a5812d9af353bd0255)
- Supports `6B.8`, `6B.10`, `6B.3`

6. **Crises still exposed vulnerable groups**
- The government’s response to poverty, food security, and climate-related risk relied on existing social-protection systems that were still being refined.
- Best anchors: [ILO adaptive social protection report](https://researchrepository.ilo.org/esploro/outputs/report/Social-insurance-and-climate-change-in/995271997402676), [BPK accountability materials]
- Supports `6B.6`, `6B.9`, `6B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `6B.1` Welfare as a governing purpose: supported, but more through priority and funding than through proof of transformation.
- `6B.10` Lasting life chances: mixed; some improvement, but not enough evidence of durable broad-based convergence.
- `6B.2` Access to essential services: partial support; access improved in some channels, but uneven delivery persisted.
- `6B.3` Priority for vulnerable groups: partial support; targeted policy was real, but outcomes remained uneven.
- `6B.4` Professional service delivery: mixed; institutions existed, but delivery quality varied.
- `6B.5` Measurement and correction: partial support; BPS and Ombudsman data enabled evaluation, but correction was inconsistent.
- `6B.6` Protection during crises: partial support; systems were present but still strain-prone.
- `6B.7` No political allocation of welfare: mixed; formal rules existed, but targeting and distribution problems remained.
- `6B.8` Dignity and equal opportunity: partial at best; formal equal-rights structure, uneven practical realization.
- `6B.9` Durable social institutions: supported in form, mixed in resilience and performance.

**Information Environment**
- The strongest evidence is from BPS, budget documents, and formal policy texts.
- The best caution is that official social-policy claims often describe intent or allocations rather than outcomes.
- A second caution is attribution: social outcomes lag budgets and depend heavily on provincial and local delivery.
- The best way to read 2023 is as continuity of a large social-policy state with persistent implementation gaps.

**Remaining Research Gaps**
- What was the actual change in child nutrition, stunting, and service uptake in 2023 by province?
- Did the 2023 social-security roadmap translate into improved coverage or only formal reorganization?
- Were poor regions, women, disabled people, and rural households measurably better served in 2023?
- Which programs were most effective at reducing deprivation versus merely redistributing transfers?
- How much of 2023 welfare performance was due to Jokowi-era design versus local administrative capacity?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 7B ---

**Orientation**
Jokowi’s personal-integrity record in 2023 is dominated less by direct proof of private enrichment than by a clear integrity crisis around family-linked succession politics, conflicts of interest, and accountability institutions. The strongest 2023 evidence is institutional and relational: the Constitutional Court age-rule episode that benefited his son’s candidacy, the later ethics finding against the court chief, and the public controversy over whether Jokowi was maintaining neutrality and transparency. There is also a weaker but relevant trail on unresolved corruption and accountability concerns during his administration, including cabinet graft cases and continuing scrutiny by auditors and anti-graft bodies.

**Compact Evidence Index**

1. **The 2023 court-age ruling created a major integrity problem**
- The Constitutional Court’s age-rule decision helped open the path for Gibran’s vice-presidential candidacy, and the court later found Anwar Usman had violated ethics rules.
- Best anchor: [MKRI ethics ruling](https://en.mkri.id/news/details/2023-11-07/Ethics_Council_Removes_Anwar_Usman_from_Chief_Justice_Position)
- Supports `7B.3`, `7B.5`, `7B.6`, `7B.7`, `7B.8`, `7B.10`

2. **Jokowi’s public denials did not fully settle the conflict-of-interest question**
- Jokowi said the age-limit judicial review was a judicial matter, but the surrounding facts kept the conflict-of-interest issue alive.
- Best anchors: [ANTARA on the judicial-review comment](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3666192/jokowi-uji-materi-batas-usia-minimum-capres-cawapres-urusan-yudikatif) and the MKRI ethics finding
- Supports `7B.1`, `7B.2`, `7B.3`, `7B.7`, `7B.8`

3. **Dynastic politics became a public integrity controversy**
- Jokowi repeatedly had to respond to accusations of political dynasty and nepotism around his sons.
- Best anchors: [ANTARA on dynasty responses](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3788892/jokowi-kembali-tanggapi-soal-dinasti-politik), [ANTARA on Gibran and nepotism](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3819729/tkn-prabowo-gibran-pemimpin-pilihan-rakyat-bukan-hasil-nepotisme)
- Supports `7B.3`, `7B.4`, `7B.5`, `7B.8`, `7B.10`

4. **There was accountability pressure, including KPK-related reporting**
- Jokowi and his sons faced reports to the KPK, and he publicly said he respected the legal process.
- Best anchors: [ANTARA on respecting the legal process](https://www.antaranews.com/berita/3788871/jokowi-hormati-laporan-ke-kpk-atas-dirinya-dan-kedua-putranya) and related video report
- Supports `7B.6`, `7B.7`, `7B.10`

5. **Anti-corruption institutions remained active, but evidence of cure is limited**
- BPK’s 2023 findings and KPK annual reporting show that accountability institutions were functioning, but the persistence of large findings and graft cases undercut claims of a clean integrity environment.
- Best anchors: [BPK 2023 findings](https://www.bpk.go.id/news/bpk-ungkap-9261-temuan-senilai-rp1819-triliun-pada-semester-i-tahun-2023), [BPK accountability release](https://www.bpk.go.id/news/bpk-sampaikan-penguatan-akuntabilitas-keuangan-negara-selama-satu-dekade-terakhir)
- Supports `7B.4`, `7B.6`, `7B.7`, `7B.9`, `7B.10`

6. **Cabinet graft scandals affected the integrity climate**
- Several Jokowi-era ministers were implicated in corruption cases, which is relevant to the ethical environment surrounding the president.
- Best anchors: earlier chapter records and Reuters-style reporting on ministerial graft
- Supports `7B.4`, `7B.5`, `7B.9`, `7B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `7B.1` Truthfulness: mixed; no single decisive lie record here, but the public record around succession and neutrality is strained.
- `7B.10` Ethical example and public trust: adverse-to-mixed; the dynastic controversy and ethics ruling damaged trust.
- `7B.2` Correcting falsehoods and errors: weak; some legal-process deference, but no strong self-correction record.
- `7B.3` Conflicts of interest: adverse; the family-linked succession episode is the central problem.
- `7B.4` Personal profit from office: inconclusive from the sources opened here; no direct proof of personal enrichment found.
- `7B.5` Nepotism and loyalist appointments: adverse in effect; family-linked advancement is the core concern.
- `7B.6` Independent investigation: mixed; institutions did investigate, but independence was under stress.
- `7B.7` Obstruction and concealment: mixed; no definitive obstruction finding here, but the surrounding record raises concern.
- `7B.8` Promises and good faith: mixed-to-adverse; neutrality and anti-dynasty assurances were strained by events.
- `7B.9` Favoritism and clientelism: mixed-to-adverse; the public record shows strong suspicion, not a clean exoneration.

**Information Environment**
- The best evidence is institutional: court ethics findings, election-law records, auditor reporting, and direct presidential statements.
- The hardest question is direct personal profit or hidden self-dealing. The opened sources do not prove that, so it should remain unresolved rather than assumed.
- The strongest integrity signal in 2023 is not personal theft, but erosion of perceived honesty and conflict-free governance.

**Remaining Research Gaps**
- Was there any direct asset, contract, or gift evidence tying Jokowi or close family to public-office profit in 2023?
- What exact role, if any, did Jokowi play in the age-rule episode beyond public commentary?
- Did KPK or BPK produce any 2023 findings directly involving the president’s office or his family?
- Were there any documented instances of retaliation against investigators, whistleblowers, or judges tied to integrity controversies?

--- CHAPTER RESEARCH 8B ---

**Orientation**
Jokowi’s 2023 effectiveness record is strongest where the goals were already well-defined and measurable: economic stabilization, downstream industrial policy, social spending, and the 2025-2045 planning transition. The year shows real administrative capacity and follow-through, but also a pattern of high-level direction with uneven implementation quality. The clearest conclusion from the 2023 record is that Jokowi could still mobilize the state toward his preferred program, but durable institutional transformation was incomplete and several signature priorities remained dependent on continued political control after his term.

**Compact Evidence Index**

1. **A coherent governing program was visible**
- Jokowi’s 2023 program was identifiable through repeated themes: downstreaming, investment, social protection, stunting reduction, and the 2045 vision.
- Best anchors: [Setkab budget and budget speech sources](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-delivers-details-of-the-2023-state-budget), [Setkab on downstreaming](https://en.antaranews.com/news/290712/no-country-can-stop-indonesias-downstreaming-policy-jokowi), [Bappenas RPJPN 2025-2045 launch](https://bappenas.go.id/en/berita/luncurkan-rancangan-akhir-rpjpn-2025-2045-presiden-paparkan-visi-indonesia-emas-2045-c29Ju)
- Supports `8B.1`, `8B.2`, `8B.6`

2. **The state was actually mobilized around that program**
- The 2023 budget continued to prioritize social protection, education, and human-capital measures.
- Best anchors: [Setkab on 2023 budget priorities](https://setkab.go.id/en/govt-continues-to-prioritize-social-protection-program-education-on-2023-state-budget), [Perpres 36/2023 social security roadmap](https://peraturan.bpk.go.id/Search?p=1&tema=118)
- Supports `8B.2`, `8B.3`, `8B.6`, `8B.9`

3. **Implementation was real, not just rhetorical**
- Growth, investment realization, and downstreaming policy were not merely symbolic: Indonesia still posted solid macro results and exceeded the investment target.
- Best anchors: [BPS GDP growth 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/pressrelease/2024/02/05/2379/ekonomi-indonesia-triwulan-iv-2023-tumbuh-5-04-persen%E2%80%93y-on-y-.html), [ANTARA investment realization](https://en.antaranews.com/news/303858/investment-realization-of-rp14189-trillion-in-2023-surpassed-target)
- Supports `8B.3`, `8B.5`, `8B.6`, `8B.7`

4. **He chose implementers and institutions that could move the agenda**
- The record shows a presidency still able to direct ministries, the central budget, and strategic planning bodies.
- Best anchors: Bappenas launch, budget documents, and the downstreaming program
- Supports `8B.4`, `8B.5`, `8B.9`

5. **Learning and correction were partial**
- Official oversight and evaluation mechanisms existed, but the record does not show a strong 2023 pattern of rapid course correction when delivery gaps appeared.
- Best anchors: Ombudsman and BPK materials, BPS publications, social-security roadmap
- Supports `8B.4`, `8B.8`, `8B.9`

6. **Durability was mixed**
- Some goals were converted into lasting law and planning, but a lot of the 2023 agenda still depended on Jokowi’s personal authority and political continuation.
- Best anchors: RPJPN 2025-2045 launch, social-security roadmap, downstreaming rhetoric
- Supports `8B.7`, `8B.10`

**Disposition by Lens**

- `8B.1` Clear governing program: supported. The 2023 program was visible and testable.
- `8B.10` Durable goal achievement: mixed. He left a stronger policy structure, but durability remains uneven.
- `8B.2` Turning goals into machinery: supported. Program goals were translated into budgets, regulations, and planning institutions.
- `8B.3` Mobilizing the ruling system: supported. The state and policy network were clearly activated.
- `8B.4` Capable implementers: partial. The system could implement major priorities, but evidence of consistently superior management is incomplete.
- `8B.5` Coordination and control: mixed. Coordination was sufficient for major macro and planning goals, weaker on distribution and local delivery.
- `8B.6` From plans to practice: supported in the macro and planning domains, weaker in service-quality domains.
- `8B.7` Progress toward chosen goals: mixed-positive. Macro growth and investment targets were met, but not all social or structural goals can be shown achieved.
- `8B.8` Learning and correction: partial. Oversight existed, but the record does not show systematic rapid correction.
- `8B.9` Managing crises and resistance: supported at a broad level. The administration kept core priorities moving despite institutional friction and political transition.

**Information Environment**
- This chapter is easier than some others because outcomes are visible in budgets, planning instruments, and national statistics.
- The main limitation is that “effectiveness” is partly a causal question: strong aggregate outcomes do not prove Jokowi personally caused them.
- The best evidence here is a mix of official strategy, budget, and statistical data, which is strong for execution but weaker for isolating personal managerial quality.
- The biggest uncertainty is the gap between national-level success and local implementation quality.

**Remaining Research Gaps**
- Which 2023 priorities were actually completed on time versus merely launched?
- How much of the macro performance came from commodity cycles and inherited momentum rather than Jokowi’s choices?
- Did the 2025-2045 planning process materially change bureaucratic behavior in 2023, or mainly set a long-run frame?
- Which ministries or regions lagged the most in executing the president’s program?
- Were there concrete 2023 policy reversals after poor implementation evidence, or mostly continuation?

--- RESEARCH CONTINUATION ROUND 1 ---

I resumed the session and expanded the source pool directly for the highest-risk gaps. I could not update `research-ledger-manifest.json` in place because the workspace is read-only and the shell path was blocked by sandbox permissions, but I did recover new traceable source-claim units below and identify where the record is now stronger versus still incomplete.

What improved in this round:
- `1B`: added independent treaty-status and disarmament-diplomacy sources, plus a contemporaneous AP report on Indonesia’s nuclear-risk rhetoric.
- `2B`: added more direct 2023 ASEAN/Myanmar primary records and a contemporaneous line that Jokowi treated 5PC as the core framework.
- `7B`: added a stronger personal-integrity nexus around the KPK complaint, the Constitutional Court ethics panel, and a public LHKPN trail for Jokowi’s wealth.
- `8B`: added the formal RPJPN launch and the 2045 “tactical vision” speeches, strengthening the program/implementation chain.

What remains structurally weak:
- `1B` still lacks any independent 2023 evidence of actual nuclear capability, command-and-control, or concealed dual-use activity.
- `2B` still lacks event-level conflict datasets showing coercive cross-border operations, which is good for an absence-of-war conclusion but still thin on contrary evidence.
- `7B` still does not show direct proof of personal enrichment or hidden asset profit from office.
- `8B` still has a gap between stated program and durable outcome attribution.

--- SUPERVISOR RESEARCH TAKEOVER ROUND 2 ---

Continuation research was completed across all eight selected chapters without scoring.

Newly recoverable evidence includes:

- `1B`: 2023 IAEA safeguards inspections; BAPETEN’s 2023 nuclear-security oversight report; BAPETEN Regulation No. 2/2023 on electronic inspection/reporting; the 2023 IAEA General Conference statement; and an IAEA–BAPETEN nuclear-security detection exercise. These strengthen safeguards, safety, institutional-capacity, and crisis-preparedness lenses, while covert-capability absence remains unproven. [BAPETEN safeguards inspection](https://bapeten.go.id/berita/inspeksi-safeguards-iaea-tahun-2023-130559), [BAPETEN nuclear-security report](https://www.bapeten.go.id/berita/laporan-kegiatan-pengawasan-keamanan-nuklir-tahun-2023-131547), [IAEA statement](https://www.iaea.org/sites/default/files/23/09/indonesia-gc67.pdf).

- `2B`: UNHCR documented 644 Rohingya arrivals in five boats by February 2023; AP reported more than 1,500 arrivals since November and December anti-refugee protests; HRW documented pushbacks, misinformation, and a December mob attack. This adds independent humanitarian and contrary evidence to the official accommodation narrative. [UNHCR](https://www.unhcr.org/id/en/news/recent-developments-concerning-rohingya-refugees-aceh-indonesia), [AP](https://apnews.com/article/3ab43261f903de7751ae0b188c45f7ee), [HRW](https://www.hrw.org/news/2024/01/16/indonesia-protect-newly-arrived-rohingya-refugees).

- `3B`: Contrary evidence remains concentrated in Papua and protest policing; the continuation confirmed the need to preserve allegation/finding distinctions and avoid nationalizing localized incidents.

- `4B`: The continuation retained competitive-election and formal-institution evidence alongside the Constitutional Court legitimacy crisis, without upgrading indirect court controversy into proof of presidential illegality.

- `5B`: BPS welfare indicators explicitly compare changes across provinces and urban/rural areas, improving the distributional evidence base. [BPS Welfare Indicators 2023](https://www.bps.go.id/en/publication/2023/11/06/7807339c2dfaed0ca8e0beaa/indikator-kesejahteraan-rakyat-2023.html).

- `6B`: BPS welfare indicators and later program-evaluation material reinforce that national welfare gains were heterogeneous and that targeting and implementation require caution.

- `7B`: BPK’s 2023 audit material records 9,261 findings worth Rp18.19 trillion and emphasizes follow-up; this supports institutional-accountability evidence, not personal enrichment. [BPK](https://www.bpk.go.id/news/bpk-discloses-9261-audit-findings-worth-rp1819-trillion-iin-the-first-semester-of-2023), [BPK follow-up reporting](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-receives-2023-audit-reports-from-bpk/). The public LHKPN trail remains a benchmark, not proof of illicit gain.

- `8B`: BPK records that the government had followed up 76% of recommendations and 47% of RPJMN-related recommendations, providing a concrete but incomplete implementation/correction measure. [Setkab audit report](https://setkab.go.id/en/president-jokowi-receives-2023-audit-reports-from-bpk/).

The evidence remains materially weakest for direct personal enrichment in `7B`, covert nuclear capability in `1B`, and shock-adjusted causal effectiveness in `5B`/`8B`. Missing evidence remains uncertainty, not adverse ruler evidence.

I could not update `research-ledger-manifest.json`: the workspace is mounted read-only and shell access failed before any write operation. No existing ledger entries or methodology mappings were altered.
